Transformed Low-Rank Parameterization Can Help Robust Generalization for Tensor Neural Networks
Andong WangRIKEN AIP andong.wang@riken.jp Chao LiRIKEN AIP chao.li@riken.jp Mingyuan BaiRIKEN AIP mingyuan.bai@riken.jp Zhong JinChina University of Petroleum-Beijing at Karamay zhongjin@cupk.edu.cn, Guoxu ZhouGuangdong University of Technology gx.zhou@gdut.edu.cn Qibin ZhaoRIKEN AIP qibin.zhao@riken.jp

Abstract
Multi-channel learning has gained significant attention in recent applications, where neural networks with t-product layers (t-NNs) have shown promising performance through novel feature mapping in the transformed domain. However, despite the practical success of t-NNs, the theoretical analysis of their generalization remains unexplored. We address this gap by deriving upper bounds on the generalization error of t-NNs in both standard and adversarial settings. Notably, it reveals that t-NNs compressed with exact transformed low-rank parameterization can achieve tighter adversarial generalization bounds compared to non-compressed models. While exact transformed low-rank weights are rare in practice, the analysis demonstrates that through adversarial training with gradient flow, highly over-parameterized t-NNs with the ReLU activation can be implicitly regularized towards a transformed low-rank parameterization under certain conditions. Moreover, this paper establishes sharp adversarial generalization bounds for t-NNs with approximately transformed low-rank weights. Our analysis highlights the potential of transformed low-rank parameterization in enhancing the robust generalization of t-NNs, offering valuable insights for further research and development.



1
Introduction

Multi-channel learning is a task to extract representations from the data with multiple channels, such as multispectral images, time series, and multi-view videos, in an efficient and robust manner
[
24
,
61
,
60
,
39
]
.
Among the methods tackling this task, neural networks with t-product layers (t-NNs, see Eq. (
5
) for a typical example)
[
36
,
12
]
came to the stage very recently with remarkable efficiency and robustness in various applications such as graph learning, remote sensing, and more
[
32
,
53
,
39
,
38
,
14
,
58
,
1
,
11
]
.
What distinguishes t-NNs from other networks is the inclusion of t-product layers, founded on the algebraic framework of tensor singular value decomposition (t-SVD)
[
19
,
61
,
60
,
40
]
.
Unlike traditional tensor decompositions, t-SVD explores the transformed low-rankness, i.e., the low-rank structure of a tensor in the transformed domain under an invertible linear transform
[
18
]
.
The imposed transform in t-product layers provides additional expressivity to neural networks, while the controllable transformed low-rank structure in t-NNs enables a flexible balance between model accuracy and robustness
[
53
,
36
,
38
]
.

Despite the impressive empirical performance of t-NNs, the theoretical foundations behind their success remain unclear.
The lack of systematic theoretical analysis hinders deeper comprehension and exploration of more effective applications and robust performance of t-NNs.
Furthermore, the inclusion of the additional transform in t-NNs renders the theoretical analysis more technically challenging compared to existing work on general neural networks
[
37
,
55
,
29
,
43
]
. To address this challenge, we establish for the first time a theoretical framework for t-NNs to understand both the standard and
robust generalization behaviors,
providing both theoretical insights and practical guidance for the efficient and robust utilization of t-NNs.
Specifically, we address the following fundamental questions:

•

Can we theoretically characterize the generalization behavior of general t-NNs?
Yes. We derive the upper bounds on the generalization error for
general
t-NNs in both standard and adversarial settings in Sec.
3
.

•

How does exact transformed low-rankness influence the robust generalization of t-NNs?

In Sec.
4.1
, our analysis shows that t-NNs with
exactly
transformed low-rank weights exhibit lower adversarial generalization bounds and require fewer samples, highlighting the benefits of transformed low-rank weights in t-NNs for improved robustness and efficiency.

•

How does adversarial learning of t-NNs affect the transformed ranks of their weight tensors?

In Sec.
4.2
, we deduce that weight tensors tend to be of transformed low-rankness
approximately
for highly over-parameterized t-NNs with ReLU activation under adversarial training with gradient flow.

•

How is robust generalization impacted by approximately transformed low-rank weight tensors in t-NNs?

In Sec.
4.3
, we establish sharp adversarial generalization bounds for t-NNs with
approximately
transformed low-rank weights by carefully bridging the gap with exact transformed low-rank parameterization. This finding again underscores the importance of incorporating transformed low-rank weights as a means to enhance the robustness of t-NNs.

2
Notations and Preliminaries

In this section, we introduce the notations and provide a concise overview of t-SVD and t-NNs, which play a central role in the subsequent analysis.

Notations.

We use lowercase, lowercase boldface, and uppercase boldface letters to denote scalars,
e.g.
,
a\in\mathbb{R}
, vectors,
e.g.
,
\textnormal{{a}}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
, and matrices,
e.g.
,
\textnormal{{A}}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}
, respectively. Following the standard notations in Ref.
[
19
]
,
a 3-way tensor of size
d\times 1\times{\sf c}
is also called a
t-vector
and denoted by underlined lowercase,
e.g.
,
\underline{\textnormal{{x}}}
, whereas a 3-way tensor of size
m\times n\times{\sf c}
is also called a
t-matrix
and denoted by underlined uppercase,
e.g.
,
{\underline{\textnormal{{X}}}}
.
We use a t-vector
\underline{\textnormal{{x}}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times 1\times{\sf c}}
to represent a multi-channel example, where
{\sf c}
denotes the number of channels and
d
is the number of features for each channel.

Given a matrix
\textnormal{{A}}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}
, its Frobenius norm (F-norm) and spectral norm are defined as
\left\|{\textnormal{{A}}}\right\|_{\textnormal{F}}:=\sqrt{\sum_{i=1}^{\min\{m,n\}}\sigma^{2}_{i}}
and
\left\|{\textnormal{{A}}}\right\|:=\max_{i}\sigma_{i}
, respectively, where

\sigma_{i},\,i=1,\cdots,\min\{m,n\}

are its singular values.
The
stable rank
of a non-zero matrix
A
is defined as the squared ratio of its F-norm and spectral norm
r_{\textnormal{{stb}}}(\textnormal{{A}}):=\left\|{\textnormal{{A}}}\right\|_{\textnormal{F}}^{2}/\left\|{\textnormal{{A}}}\right\|^{2}
.
Given a tensor

{\underline{\textnormal{{T}}}}
, define its
l_{p}
-norm and F-norm respectively as
{\left\|{{\underline{\textnormal{{T}}}}}\right\|_{l_{p}}:=\left\|{\textnormal{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\footnotesize\small{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{vec}}}}}}}({\underline{\textnormal{{T}}}})}\right\|_{l_{p}}
, and
{\left\|{{\underline{\textnormal{{T}}}}}\right\|_{\textnormal{F}}:=\left\|{\textnormal{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\footnotesize\small{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{vec}}}}}}}({\underline{\textnormal{{T}}}})}\right\|_{l_{2}}
,
where
{\textnormal{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\footnotesize\small{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{vec}}}}}}}(\cdot)
denotes the vectorization operation of a tensor

[
21
]
.
Given
{\underline{\textnormal{{T}}}}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n\times{\sf c}}
, let
{\underline{\textnormal{{T}}}}_{:,:,i}
denote its
i^{\textnormal{th}}
frontal slice.
The inner
product between two tensors
{\underline{\textnormal{{A}}}},{\underline{\textnormal{{B}}}}
is
defined as

{{\left\langle{\underline{\textnormal{{A}}}},{\underline{\textnormal{{B}}}}\right\rangle:={\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\footnotesize\small{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{vec}}}}}}}({\underline{\textnormal{{A}}}})^{\top}{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\footnotesize\small{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{vec}}}}}}}({\underline{\textnormal{{B}}}}).

The frontal-slice-wise product of two tensors
{\underline{\textnormal{{A}}}},{\underline{\textnormal{{B}}}}
, denoted by
{\underline{\textnormal{{A}}}}\odot{\underline{\textnormal{{B}}}}
, equals a tensor
{\underline{\textnormal{{T}}}}
such that

{\underline{\textnormal{{T}}}}_{:,:,i}={\underline{\textnormal{{A}}}}_{:,:,i}{\underline{\textnormal{{B}}}}_{:,:,i},\penalty\ i=1,\cdots,{\sf c}

[
19
]
. We use
|\cdot|
as the absolute value for a scalar and cardinality for a set. We use
\circ
to denote the function composition operation.
Additional notations will be introduced upon their first occurrence.

2.1
Tensor Singular Value Decomposition

The framework of tensor singular value decomposition (t-SVD) is based on the t-product under an invertible linear transform
M

[
18
]
.
In recent studies, the transformation matrix
M
defining the transform
M
is
restricted to be orthogonal

[
50
]
for better properties, which is also followed in this paper.
Given any
orthogonal matrix

\textnormal{{M}}\in\mathbb{R}^{{\sf c}\times{\sf c}}
, define the associated linear transform
M(\cdot)
with its inverse
M^{-1}(\cdot)
on any
{\underline{\textnormal{{T}}}}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n\times{\sf c}}
as

\displaystyle\penalty\ M({\underline{\textnormal{{T}}}}):={\underline{\textnormal{{T}}}}\times_{3}\textnormal{{M}},\penalty\ \penalty\ \textnormal{and}\quad M^{-1}({\underline{\textnormal{{T}}}}):={\underline{\textnormal{{T}}}}\times_{3}\textnormal{{M}}^{-1},

(1)

where
\times_{3}
denotes the tensor matrix product on mode-
3

[
18
]
.

Definition 1

(t-product
[
18
]
)
.

The t-product of any
{\underline{\textnormal{{A}}}}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n\times{\sf c}}
and
{\underline{\textnormal{{B}}}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k\times{\sf c}}
under
transform
M
in Eq. (
1
) is denoted and defined as
{\underline{\textnormal{{A}}}}*_{M}{\underline{\textnormal{{B}}}}
=
{\underline{\textnormal{{C}}}}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times k\times{\sf c}}
such that
M({\underline{\textnormal{{C}}}})=M({\underline{\textnormal{{A}}}})\odot M({\underline{\textnormal{{B}}}})
in the transformed domain. Equivalently, we have
{\underline{\textnormal{{C}}}}=M^{-1}(M({\underline{\textnormal{{A}}}})\odot M({\underline{\textnormal{{B}}}}))
in the original domain.

Definition 2

(
M
-block-diagonal matrix)
.

The
M
-block-diagonal matrix of any
{\underline{\textnormal{{T}}}}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n\times{\sf c}}
, denoted by
\widetilde{\textnormal{{T}}}_{M}
, is the block diagonal matrix whose diagonal blocks are the frontal slices of
M({\underline{\textnormal{{T}}}})
:

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\penalty\ \widetilde{\textnormal{{T}}}_{M}:=\textnormal{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\footnotesize\small{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{bdiag}}}}}}}(M({\underline{\textnormal{{T}}}})):={\small\begin{bmatrix}M({\underline{\textnormal{{T}}}})_{:,:,1}&&&\\
&M({\underline{\textnormal{{T}}}})_{:,:,2}&&\\
&&\ddots&\\
&&&M({\underline{\textnormal{{T}}}})_{:,:,{\sf c}}\end{bmatrix}}\in\mathbb{R}^{m{\sf c}\times n{\sf c}}.

In this paper, we also follow the definition of t-transpose, t-identity tensor, t-orthogonal tensor, and f-diagonal tensor given by Ref.
[
18
]
, and thus the t-SVD is introduced as follows.

Definition 3

(t-SVD, tubal rank
[
18
]
)
.

Tensor Singular Value Decomposition (t-SVD) of
{\underline{\textnormal{{T}}}}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n\times{\sf c}}
under the invertible linear transform
M
in Eq. (
1
) is given as follows

\displaystyle\penalty\ {\underline{\textnormal{{T}}}}={\underline{\textnormal{{U}}}}*_{M}{\underline{\textnormal{{S}}}}*_{M}{\underline{\textnormal{{V}}}}^{\top},

(2)

where
{\underline{\textnormal{{U}}}}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times m\times{\sf c}}
and
{\underline{\textnormal{{V}}}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n\times{\sf c}}
are t-orthogonal, and
{\underline{\textnormal{{S}}}}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n\times{\sf c}}
is f-diagonal.
The tubal rank of
{\underline{\textnormal{{T}}}}
is
defined as the number of non-zero tubes of

{\underline{\textnormal{{S}}}}
in its
t-SVD in Eq. (
2
),
i.e.,

r_{\textnormal{t}}({\underline{\textnormal{{T}}}}):=|\{i\penalty\ |\penalty\ {\underline{\textnormal{{S}}}}(i,i,:)\neq\textnormal{{0}},i\leq\min\{m,n\}\}|.

For any
{\underline{\textnormal{{T}}}}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n\times{\sf c}}
with the tubal rank
r_{t}({\underline{\textnormal{{T}}}})
, we have following relationship between its t-SVD and the matrix SVD of its
M
-block-diagonal matrix
[
50
,
26
]
:

\displaystyle\penalty\ \penalty\ {\underline{\textnormal{{T}}}}={\underline{\textnormal{{U}}}}*_{M}{\underline{\textnormal{{S}}}}*_{M}{\underline{\textnormal{{V}}}}^{\top}\penalty\ \Leftrightarrow\penalty\ \widetilde{\textnormal{{T}}}_{M}=\widetilde{\textnormal{{U}}}_{M}\cdot\widetilde{\textnormal{{S}}}_{M}\cdot\widetilde{\textnormal{{V}}}_{M}^{\top},\quad\textnormal{and}\qquad{\sf c}\cdot r_{\textnormal{t}}({\underline{\textnormal{{T}}}})\geq\rank(\widetilde{\textnormal{{T}}}_{M}).

(3)

As the
M
-block-diagonal matrix
\widetilde{\textnormal{{T}}}_{M}
is defined after transforming tensor
{\underline{\textnormal{{T}}}}
from the original domain to the transformed domain, the relationship
{\sf c}\cdot r_{\textnormal{t}}({\underline{\textnormal{{T}}}})\geq\rank(\widetilde{\textnormal{{T}}}_{M})
indicates that the tubal rank can be chosen as a measure of transformed low-rankness
[
50
,
26
]
.

2.2
Neural Networks with t-Product Layer (t-NNs)

In this subsection, we will introduce the formulation of the t-product layer in t-NNs, which is designed for multi-channel feature learning.

Multi-channel feature learning via t-product.

Suppose we have a multi-channel example represented by a t-vector
\underline{\textnormal{{x}}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times 1\times{\sf c}}
, where
{\sf c}
is the number of channels and
d
is the number of features. We define an
L
-layer t-NN feature extractor
\textnormal{{f}}(\underline{\textnormal{{x}}})
, to extract
d_{L}
features for each channel of
\underline{\textnormal{{x}}}
:

\displaystyle\penalty\ \textnormal{{f}}(\underline{\textnormal{{x}}})=\textnormal{{f}}^{(L)}(\underline{\textnormal{{x}}});\quad\textnormal{{f}}^{(l)}(\underline{\textnormal{{x}}})=\sigma({\underline{\textnormal{{W}}}}^{(l)}*_{M}\textnormal{{f}}^{(l-1)}(\underline{\textnormal{{x}}})),\penalty\ l=1,\cdots,L;\quad\textnormal{{f}}^{(0)}(\underline{\textnormal{{x}}})=\underline{\textnormal{{x}}},

(4)

where the
l
-th layer
\textnormal{{f}}^{(l)}
first conducts t-product with weight tensor (t-matrix)
{\underline{\textnormal{{W}}}}^{(l)}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{l}\times d_{l-1}\times{\sf c}}
on the output of the
(l-1)
-th layer as multi-channel features
1
1

1

For simplicity, let
d_{0}=d
by treating the input example
\underline{\textnormal{{x}}}
as the
0
-th layer
\textnormal{{f}}^{(0)}
.

\textnormal{{f}}^{(l-1)}(\underline{\textnormal{{x}}})\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{l-1}\times 1\times{\sf c}}
to obtain a (
d_{l}\times 1\times{\sf c}
)-dimensional representation and then uses the entry-wisely ReLU activation
2
2

2

Although we consider ReLU activation in this paper, most of the main theoretical results (
e.g.
, Theorems
3
,
5
,
6
,
12
, and
14
) can be generalized to general Lipschitz activations with slight modifications in the proof.

\sigma(x)=\max\{x,0\}
for nonlinearity.

Remark
.

Unlike Refs.
[
36
]
,
[
32
]
and
[
53
]
whose nonlinear activation is performed in the transformed domain, the t-NN model in Eq. (
4
) considers the nonlinear activation in the original domain and hence is consistent with traditional neural networks.

By adding a linear classification module with weight
\textnormal{{w}}\in\mathbb{R}^{cd_{L}}
after the feature exaction module in Eq. (
4
), we consider the following t-NN predictor whose sign can be utilized for binary classification:

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\penalty\ f(\underline{\textnormal{{x}}};{\underline{\textnormal{{W}}}}):=\textnormal{{w}}^{\top}\textnormal{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\footnotesize\small{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{vec}}}}}}}(\textnormal{{f}}^{(L)}(\underline{\textnormal{{x}}}))\in\mathbb{R}.

(5)

Let
{\underline{\textnormal{{W}}}}:=\{{\underline{\textnormal{{W}}}}^{(1)},\cdots,{\underline{\textnormal{{W}}}}^{(L)},\textnormal{{w}}\}
be the collection of all the weights
3
3

3

Here for the ease of notation presentation, we use the tensor notation
{\underline{\textnormal{{W}}}}
instead of the set notation
\mathcal{W}
.
. With a slight abuse of notation, let
\left\|{{\underline{\textnormal{{W}}}}}\right\|_{\textnormal{F}}:=\sqrt{\left\|{\textnormal{{w}}}\right\|_{2}^{2}+\sum_{l=1}^{L}\|{\underline{\textnormal{{W}}}}^{(l)}\|_{\textnormal{F}}^{2}}
denote the Euclidean norm of all the weights.
The function class of general t-NNs whose weights are bounded in the Euclidean norm is defined as

\displaystyle\penalty\ \mathfrak{F}:=\left\{f(\textnormal{{x}};{\underline{\textnormal{{W}}}})\penalty\ \textnormal{ }\penalty\ \big|\penalty\ \left\|{\textnormal{{w}}}\right\|_{2}\leq B_{w},\quad\|{\underline{\textnormal{{W}}}}^{(l)}\|_{\textnormal{F}}\leq B_{l},\quad l=1,\cdots,L\right\},

(6)

with positive constants
B_{w}
and
B_{l},\penalty\ l=1,\cdots,L
.
Let
B_{{\underline{\textnormal{{W}}}}}:=B_{w}\prod_{l=1}^{L}B_{l}
for simplicity.

3
Standard and Robust Generalization Bounds for t-NNs

This section establishes both the standard and robust generalization bounds for any t-NN
f\in\mathfrak{F}
.

3.1
Standard Generalization for General t-NNs

Suppose we are given a training multi-channel dataset
S
consisting of
N
example-label pairs
\{(\underline{\textnormal{{x}}}_{i},y_{i})\}^{N}_{i=1}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d\times 1\times{\sf c}}\times\{\pm 1\}

i.i.d.
drawn from an underlying data distribution
P_{\underline{\textnormal{{x}}},y}
.

Assumption 1
.

Every input example
\underline{\textnormal{{x}}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times 1\times c}
has an upper bounded
F-norm, i.e.,
\left\|{\underline{\textnormal{{x}}}}\right\|_{\textnormal{F}}\leq B_{x}
, where
B_{x}
is a positive constant.

When a loss function
\ell(f(\underline{\textnormal{{x}}}_{i}),y_{i})
is considered as the measure of the classification quality, we define the empirical and population risk for any
f\in\mathfrak{F}
as

\hat{\mathcal{L}}(f):=N^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\ell(f(\underline{\textnormal{{x}}}_{i}),y_{i})
and
\mathcal{L}(f):=\mathbb{E}_{P_{(\underline{\textnormal{{x}}},y)}}\left[\ell(f(\underline{\textnormal{{x}}}),y)\right],

respectively.
Similar to Ref.
[
30
]
, we make assumptions on the loss as follows.

Assumption 2
.

The loss
\ell(h(\underline{\textnormal{{x}}}),y)
can be expressed as
\ell(h(\underline{\textnormal{{x}}}),y)=\exp(-{\mathfrak{f}}(yh(\underline{\textnormal{{x}}}))
for any t-NN
h\in\mathfrak{F}
, such that:

(A.1)

the range of loss
\ell(\cdot,\cdot)
is
[0,B]
, where
B
is a positive constant;

(A.2)

function
{\mathfrak{f}}:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is
C^{1}
-smooth;

(A.3)

{\mathfrak{f}}^{\prime}(x)\geq 0
for any
x\in\mathbb{R}
;

(A.4)

there exists
b_{{\mathfrak{f}}}\geq 0
such that
x{\mathfrak{f}}^{\prime}(x)
is non-decreasing for
x\in(b_{{\mathfrak{f}}},+\infty)
, and the derivative
x{\mathfrak{f}}^{\prime}(x)\rightarrow+\infty
as
x\rightarrow+\infty
;

(A.5)

let
{\mathfrak{g}}:[{\mathfrak{f}}(b_{{\mathfrak{f}}}),+\infty)\rightarrow[b_{{\mathfrak{f}}},+\infty)
be the inverse function of
{\mathfrak{f}}
on the domain
[b_{{\mathfrak{f}}},+\infty)
. There exist
b_{{\mathfrak{g}}}\geq\max\{2{\mathfrak{f}}(b_{{\mathfrak{f}}}),{\mathfrak{f}}(2b_{{\mathfrak{f}}})\}
and
K\geq 1
, such that

{\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime}(x)\leq K{\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime}(\theta x)
and
{\mathfrak{f}}^{\prime}(y)\leq K{\mathfrak{f}}^{\prime}(\theta y)
for any
x\in(b_{{\mathfrak{g}}},+\infty),y\in({\mathfrak{g}}(b_{{\mathfrak{g}}}),+\infty)
and
\theta\in[1/2,1)
.

Assumption
(A.1)
is a natural assumption in generalization analysis
[
59
,
3
]
, and Assumptions
(A.2)-(A.5)
are the same as Assumption (B3) in Ref.
[
30
]
.
According to Assumption
(A.2)
, the loss function
\ell(\cdot,\cdot)
satisfies the
L_{\ell}
-Lipschitz continuity

\displaystyle\penalty\ |\ell(h(\underline{\textnormal{{x}}}_{1}),y_{1})-\ell(h(\underline{\textnormal{{x}}}_{2}),y_{2})|\leq L_{\ell}|y_{1}h(\underline{\textnormal{{x}}}_{1})-y_{2}(\underline{\textnormal{{x}}}_{2})|,\quad\textnormal{with}\penalty\ L_{\ell}=\sup\nolimits_{|q|\leq B_{\tilde{f}}}{\mathfrak{f}}^{\prime}(q)e^{-{\mathfrak{f}}(q)},

(7)

where
B_{\tilde{f}}
is an upper bound on the output of any t-NN
h\in\mathfrak{F}
.
The Lipschitz continuity is also widely assumed for generalization analysis of DNNs
[
59
,
55
]
.
Assumption
2
is satisfied by commonly used loss functions such as the logistic loss and the exponential loss.

The generalization gap
\mathcal{L}(f)-\hat{\mathcal{L}}(f)
of any function
f\in\mathfrak{F}
can be bounded as follows.

Lemma 3

(Generalization bound for t-NNs)
.

Under Assumptions
1
and
2
,
it holds for any
f\in\mathfrak{F}
that

\displaystyle\penalty\ \mathcal{L}(f)-\hat{\mathcal{L}}(f)\leq\frac{L_{\ell}B_{x}B_{{\underline{\textnormal{{W}}}}}}{\sqrt{N}}(\sqrt{2\log(2(L+1))}+1)+3B\sqrt{\frac{t}{2N}},

(8)

with probability at least
1-2e^{-t}
for any
t>0
.

Remark
.

When the input example has channel number
{\sf c}=1
, the generalization bound in Theorem
3
is consistent with the F-norm-based bound in Ref.
[
8
]
.

3.2
Robust Generalization for General t-NNs

We study the adversarial generalization behavior of t-NNs in this section. We first make the following assumption on the adversarial perturbations.

Assumption 4
.

Given an input example
\underline{\textnormal{{x}}}
, the adversarial perturbation is chosen within a radius-
\xi
ball of norm
R_{\textnormal{a}}(\cdot)
with compatibility constant
[
35
]
defined as

{\sf C}_{R_{\textnormal{a}}}:=\sup_{\underline{\textnormal{{x}}}\neq\textnormal{{0}}}R_{\textnormal{a}}(\underline{\textnormal{{x}}})/\left\|{\underline{\textnormal{{x}}}}\right\|_{\textnormal{F}}
.

The assumption allows for much broader adversary classes than the commonly considered
l_{p}
-attacks
[
54
,
55
]
. For example, if one treats the multi-channel data
\underline{\textnormal{{x}}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times 1\times{\sf c}}
as a matrix of dimensionality
d\times{\sf c}
and attacks it with nuclear norm attacks
[
17
]
, then the constant
{\sf C}_{R_{\textnormal{a}}}=\sqrt{\min\{d,{\sf c}\}}
.

Given an example-label pair
(\underline{\textnormal{{x}}},y)
, the adversarial loss for any predictor
f
is defined as

\tilde{\ell}(f(\underline{\textnormal{{x}}}),y)=\max_{R_{\textnormal{a}}(\underline{\textnormal{{x}}}^{\prime}-\underline{\textnormal{{x}}})\leq\xi}\ell(f(\underline{\textnormal{{x}}}^{\prime}),y).

The empirical and population adversarial risks are thus defined
as

\hat{\mathcal{L}}^{\textnormal{adv}}(f):=N^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\tilde{\ell}(f(\underline{\textnormal{{x}}}_{i}),y_{i})
and
\mathcal{L}^{\textnormal{adv}}(f):=\mathbb{E}_{P_{(\underline{\textnormal{{x}}},y)}}[\tilde{\ell}(f(\underline{\textnormal{{x}}}),y)]
,
respectively.
The adversarial generalization performance is measured by the adversarial generalization gap (AGP) defined as

\mathcal{L}^{\textnormal{adv}}(f)-\hat{\mathcal{L}}^{\textnormal{adv}}(f)
.
Let
B_{\tilde{f}}:=(B_{x}+\xi{\sf C}_{R_{\textnormal{a}}})B_{{\underline{\textnormal{{W}}}}}
. For any
f\in\mathfrak{F}
, its AGP is bounded as follows.

Theorem 5

(Adversarial generalization bound for t-NNs)
.

Under Assumptions
1
,
2
, and
4
, there exists a constant
C
such that
for any
f\in\mathfrak{F}
, it holds with probability at least
1-2e^{-t}\penalty\ (\forall t>0)
:

\displaystyle\penalty\ \mathcal{L}^{\textnormal{adv}}(f)-\hat{\mathcal{L}}^{\textnormal{adv}}(f)\leq

\displaystyle\frac{CL_{\ell}B_{\tilde{f}}}{\sqrt{N}}\sqrt{{\sf c}\sum_{l=1}^{L}d_{l-1}d_{l}\log(3(L+1))}+3B\sqrt{\frac{t}{2N}}.

(9)

Remark
.

When the input example has channel number
{\sf c}=1
and the attacker uses
l_{p}
-attack, the adversarial generalization bound in Theorem
5
degenerates to the one in Theorem 4 of Ref.
[
55
]
.

4
Transformed Low-rank Parameterization for Robust Generalization

4.1
Robust Generalization with Exact Transformed Low-rank Parameterization

According to Theorem
5
, the AGP bound scales with the square root of the parameter complexity, specifically as
O(\sqrt{{\sf c}(\sum_{l}d_{l-1}d_{l})/N})
. This implies that achieving the desired adversarial accuracy may require a large number
N
of training examples. Furthermore, high parameter complexity leads to increased energy consumption, storage requirements, and computational cost when deploying large t-NN models, particularly on resource-constrained embedded and mobile devices.

To this end, we propose a transformed low-rank parameterization scheme to compress the original t-NN models
\mathfrak{F}
. Specifically, given a vector of pre-set ranks
\textnormal{{r}}=(r_{1},\cdots,r_{L})^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{L}
where
r_{l}\leq\min\{d_{l},d_{l-1}\}
, we consider the following subset of the original t-NNs:

\displaystyle\penalty\ \mathfrak{F}_{\textnormal{{r}}}:=\Big\{f\penalty\ \big|\penalty\ f\in\mathfrak{F},\textnormal{and}\penalty\ r_{\textnormal{t}}({\underline{\textnormal{{W}}}}^{(l)})\leq r_{l},\penalty\ \penalty\ l=1,\cdots,L\Big\}.

(10)

In the function set
\mathfrak{F}_{\textnormal{{r}}}
, the weight tensor
{\underline{\textnormal{{W}}}}^{(l)}
of the
l
-th layer has the upper bounded tubal rank, which means low-rankness in the transformed domain
4
4

4

For empirical implementations, one can adopt similar rank learning strategy to Ref.
[
15
]
to select a suitable rank parameter
r
. Due to the scope of this paper, we leave this for future work.
.
We bound the AGP for any
f\in\mathfrak{F}_{\textnormal{{r}}}
as follows.

Theorem 6

(Adversarial generalization bound for t-NNs with transformed low-rank weights)
.

Under Assumptions
1
,
2
, and
4
, there exists a constant
C^{\prime}
such that

\displaystyle\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathcal{L}^{\textnormal{adv}}(f_{\textnormal{{r}}})-\hat{\mathcal{L}}^{\textnormal{adv}}(f_{\textnormal{{r}}})\leq\frac{C^{\prime}L_{\ell}B_{\tilde{f}}}{\sqrt{N}}\sqrt{{\sf c}\sum_{l=1}^{L}r_{l}(d_{l-1}+d_{l})\log(9(L+1))}+3B\sqrt{\frac{t}{2N}},

(11)

holds for any
f_{\textnormal{{r}}}\in\mathfrak{F}_{\textnormal{{r}}}
with probability at least
1-2e^{-t}\penalty\ (\forall t>0)
.

Comparing Theorem
6
with Theorem
5
, we observe that the adversarial generalization bound under transformed low-rank parameterization has a better scaling, specifically
O(\sqrt{{\sf c}\sum_{l}r_{l}(d_{l-1}+d_{l})/N})
. This also implies that a smaller number
N
of training examples is required to achieve the desired accuracy, as well as reduced energy consumption, storage requirements, and computational cost. Please refer to Sec.
A.1
in the appendix for numerical evidence.

4.2
Implicit Bias of Gradient Flow for Adversarial Training of Over-parameterized t-NNs

Although Theorem
6
shows
exactly
transformed low-rank parameterization leads to lower bounds, the well trained t-NNs on real data rarely have exactly transformed low-rank weights. In this section, we prove that the highly over-parameterized t-NNs, trained by adversarial training with gradient flow (GF), are
approximately
of transformed low-rank parameterization under certain conditions.

First, the proposed t-NN
f(\underline{\textnormal{{x}}};{\underline{\textnormal{{W}}}})
is said to be (positively)
homogeneous
as the condition

f(\underline{\textnormal{{x}}};a{\underline{\textnormal{{W}}}})=a^{L+1}f(\underline{\textnormal{{x}}};{\underline{\textnormal{{W}}}})

holds for any positive constant
a
.
Motivated by Ref.
[
29
]
, we focus on the scale invariant adversarial perturbations defined as follows.

Definition 4

(Scale invariant adversarial perturbation
[
29
]
)
.

An adversarial perturbation
\underline{\mathbf{\delta}}_{i}({\underline{\textnormal{{W}}}})
is said to be scale invariant for
f(\underline{\textnormal{{x}}};{\underline{\textnormal{{W}}}})
at any given example
\underline{\textnormal{{x}}}_{i}
if it satisfies

\underline{\mathbf{\delta}}_{i}(a{\underline{\textnormal{{W}}}})=\underline{\mathbf{\delta}}_{i}({\underline{\textnormal{{W}}}})
for any positive constant
a
.

Lemma 7
.

The
l_{2}
-FGM
[
34
]
, FGSM
[
9
]
,
l_{2}
-PGD and
l_{\infty}
-PGD
[
31
]
perturbations for the t-NNs are all scale invariant.

Then, we consider adversarial training of t-NNs with scale invariant adversarial perturbations by GF,
which can be seen as gradient descent with infinitesimal step size.
When using GF for the ReLU t-NNs,
{\underline{\textnormal{{W}}}}
changes continuously with time, and the trajectory of parameter
{\underline{\textnormal{{W}}}}
during training is an arc
{{\underline{\textnormal{{W}}}}:[0,\infty)\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\footnotesize\small{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{dim}}}}}}({\underline{\textnormal{{W}}}})},t\mapsto{\underline{\textnormal{{W}}}}(t)
that satisfies the differential inclusion
[
7
,
30
]

\displaystyle\penalty\ \frac{{\sf d}{\underline{\textnormal{{W}}}}(t)}{{\sf d}t}\in-\partial^{\circ}\hat{\mathcal{L}}^{\textnormal{adv}}({\underline{\textnormal{{W}}}}(t))

(12)

for
t\geq 0

a.e.
,
where
\partial^{\circ}\hat{\mathcal{L}}^{\textnormal{adv}}
denotes the Clarke’s subdifferential
[
7
]
with respect to
{\underline{\textnormal{{W}}}}(t)
.
If
\hat{\mathcal{L}}^{\textnormal{adv}}({{\underline{\textnormal{{W}}}}})
is actually a
C^{1}
-smooth function, the
above differential inclusion reduces to

\displaystyle\penalty\ \frac{{\sf d}{\underline{\textnormal{{W}}}}(t)}{{\sf d}t}=-{\frac{\partial{\hat{\mathcal{L}}^{\textnormal{adv}}({\underline{\textnormal{{W}}}}(t))}}{\partial{\underline{\textnormal{{W}}}}(t)}}

(13)

for any
t\geq 0
, which corresponds to the
GF with differential in the usual sense.
However, for simplicity, we follow Refs.
[
46
,
45
]
and still use Eq. (
13
) to denote Eq. (
12
) with a slight abuse of notation, even if
\hat{\mathcal{L}}^{\textnormal{adv}}
does not satisfy differentiability but only local Lipschitzness
5
5

5

Note that the ReLU function is not differentiable at
0
.
Practical implementations of gradient methods define the derivative
\sigma^{\prime}(0)
to be a constant
in
[0,1]
. In this work we assume for convenience that
\sigma^{\prime}(0)=0
.
.

We also make an assumption on the training data as follows.

Assumption 8

(Existence of a separability of adversarial examples during training)
.

There exists a time
t_{0}
such that
\hat{\mathcal{L}}^{\textnormal{adv}}(t_{0})\leq N^{-1}\ell(b_{{\mathfrak{f}}})
.

This assumption is a generalization of the separability condition in Refs.
[
30
,
29
]
.
Adversarial training can typically achieve this
separability in practice,
i.e.
, the model can fit adversarial examples of the training dataset,
making the above assumption reasonable. Then, we obtain the following lemma.

Lemma 9

(Convergence to the direction of a KKT point)
.

Consider the hypothesis class
\mathfrak{F}
in Eq. (
6
).
Under Assumptions
2
and
8
, the limit point of normalized weights
\{{\underline{\textnormal{{W}}}}(t)/\left\|{{\underline{\textnormal{{W}}}}(t)}\right\|_{\textnormal{F}}:t\geq 0\}
of the GF for Eq. (
13
), i.e., the empirical adversarial risk with scale invariant adversarial perturbations
\underline{\mathbf{\delta}}_{i}({\underline{\textnormal{{W}}}})
, is aligned with the direction of a KKT point of the minimization problem:

\displaystyle\penalty\ \min_{{\underline{\textnormal{{W}}}}}\frac{1}{2}\left\|{{\underline{\textnormal{{W}}}}}\right\|_{\textnormal{F}}^{2},\qquad\textnormal{s.t.}\penalty\ y_{i}f(\underline{\textnormal{{x}}}_{i}+\underline{\mathbf{\delta}}_{i}({\underline{\textnormal{{W}}}});{\underline{\textnormal{{W}}}})\geq 1,\quad i=1,\cdots,N.

(14)

Building upon Lemma
9
, we can establish that highly over-parameterized t-NNs undergoing adversarial training with GF will exhibit an implicit bias towards transformed low-rank weights.

Theorem 10

(Implicit low-rankness for t-NNs induced by GF)
.

Suppose there is an example
\underline{\textnormal{{x}}}_{i}
satisfying
\left\|{\underline{\textnormal{{x}}}_{i}}\right\|_{\textnormal{F}}\leq 1
in the training set

S=\{(\underline{\textnormal{{x}}}_{i},y_{i})\}_{i=1}^{N}
.
Suppose there is a
(J+1)
-layer (
J\geq 2
) ReLU t-NN, denoted by
g(\underline{\textnormal{{x}}};{\underline{\textnormal{{V}}}})
with parameters
{\underline{\textnormal{{V}}}}=({\underline{\textnormal{{V}}}}^{(1)},\cdots,{\underline{\textnormal{{V}}}}^{(J)},\textnormal{{v}})
, satisfying the conditions:

(C.1)

the dimensionality of the weight tensor
{\underline{\textnormal{{V}}}}^{(j)}\in\mathbb{R}^{m_{j}\times m_{j-1}\times{\sf c}}
of the
j
-th t-product layer satisfies
m_{j}\geq 2
,
j=1,\cdots,J
;

(C.2)

there is a constant
B_{v}>0
, such that the Euclidean norm of the weights
{\underline{\textnormal{{V}}}}=({\underline{\textnormal{{V}}}}^{(1)},\cdots,{\underline{\textnormal{{V}}}}^{(L)},\textnormal{{v}})
satisfy
\|{\underline{\textnormal{{V}}}}^{(j)}\|_{\textnormal{F}}\leq B_{v}
for any
j=1,\cdots,J
and
\left\|{\textnormal{{v}}}\right\|_{2}\leq B_{v}
;

(C.3)

for all
i\in\{1,\cdots,N\}
, we have
y_{i}g(\underline{\textnormal{{x}}}_{i}+\underline{\mathbf{\delta}}_{i}({\underline{\textnormal{{V}}}});{\underline{\textnormal{{V}}}})\geq 1
.

Then, we consider the class of over-parameterized t-NNs
\mathfrak{F}=\{f(\underline{\textnormal{{x}}};{\underline{\textnormal{{W}}}})\}
defined in Eq. (
5
) satisfying

(C.4)

the number
L
of t-product layers is much greater than
J
;

(C.5)

the dimensionality of weight
{\underline{\textnormal{{W}}}}^{(l)}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{l}\times d_{l-1}\times{\sf c}}
satisfies
d_{l}\gg\max_{j\leq J}\{m_{j}\}
for any
l\leq L
.

Let
{\underline{\textnormal{{W}}}}^{*}=({\underline{\textnormal{{W}}}}^{*(1)},\cdots,{\underline{\textnormal{{W}}}}^{*(L)},\textnormal{{w}}^{*})
be a global optimum of Problem (
14
). Namely,
{\underline{\textnormal{{W}}}}^{*}
parameterizes a minimum-norm t-NN
f(\underline{\textnormal{{x}}};{\underline{\textnormal{{W}}}}^{*})\in\mathfrak{F}
that labels the perturbed training set correctly with margin 1 under scale invariant adversarial perturbations. Then, we have

\displaystyle\penalty\ \frac{L}{\sum_{l=1}^{L}\left(r_{\textnormal{{stb}}}(\widetilde{\textnormal{{W}}}_{M}^{*(l)})\right)^{-1/2}}\leq\frac{1}{\left(1+\frac{1}{L}\right)\left(\frac{1}{B_{v}}\right)^{\frac{J+1}{L+1}}\sqrt{\frac{L+1}{(J+1)+({\sf c}m_{J})(L-J)}}-\frac{1}{L}},

where
\widetilde{\textnormal{{W}}}_{M}^{*(l)}
denotes the
M
-block-diagonal matrix of weight tensor
{\underline{\textnormal{{W}}}}^{*(l)}
for any
l=1,\cdots,L
.

By the above theorem, when
L
is sufficiently large, the harmonic mean of the square root of the stable rank of
\widetilde{\textnormal{{W}}}_{M}^{*(l)}
,
i.e.
, the
M
-block-diagonal matrix of weight tensor
{\underline{\textnormal{{W}}}}^{*(l)}
,
is approximately bounded by
\sqrt{{\sf c}m_{J}}
, which is significantly smaller than the square root of the dimensionality
\sqrt{\min\{{\sf c}d_{l},{\sf c}d_{l-1}\}}
according to condition
(C.5)
in Theorem
10
. Thus,
f(\underline{\textnormal{{x}}};{\underline{\textnormal{{W}}}}^{*})
has a nearly low-rank parameterization in the transformed domain.
In our case, the weights
{\underline{\textnormal{{W}}}}(t)
generated by GF tend to have an infinite norm and to converge in direction to a transformed low-rank solution. Moreover, note that the ratio between the spectral norm and the F-norm is invariant to scaling, and hence it suggests that after a sufficiently long time, GF tends to reach a t-NN with transformed low-rank weight tensors. Refer to Sec.
A.2
for numerical evidence supporting Theorem
10
.

4.3
Robust Generalization with Approximate Transformed Low-rank Parameterization

Theorem
10
establishes that for highly over-parameterized adversarial training with GF, well-trained t-NNs exhibit approximately transformed low-rank parameters under specific conditions. In this section, we analyze the AGP of t-NNs that possess an approximately transformed low-rank parameterization
6
6

6

We use the tubal rank as a measure of low-rankness in the transformed domain for notation simplicity. One can also consider the average rank
[
52
]
or multi-rank
[
50
]
for more refined bounds with quite similar techniques.
.

Initially, by employing low-tubal-rank tensor approximation
[
20
]
, one can always compress an
approximately
low-tubal-rank parameterized t-NN
f
by a t-NN

g\in\mathfrak{F}_{\textnormal{{r}}}
with an
exact
low-tubal-rank parameterization, ensuring a small distance between
g
and
f
in the parameter space. Now, the question is:
Can the small parametric distance between
f
and
g
also indicate a small difference in their adversarial generalization behaviors?

To answer this question, we first define the
(\delta,\textnormal{{r}})
-approximate low-tubal-rank parameterized functions.

Definition 5

(
(\delta,\textnormal{{r}})
-approximate low-tubal-rank parameterization)
.

A t-NN
f(\underline{\textnormal{{x}}};{\underline{\textnormal{{W}}}})\in\mathfrak{F}
with weights
{\underline{\textnormal{{W}}}}=(\textnormal{{w}},{\underline{\textnormal{{W}}}}^{(1)},\cdots,{\underline{\textnormal{{W}}}}^{(L)})
is said to satisfy the
(\delta,\textnormal{{r}})
-approximate low-tubal parameterization with tolerance
\delta>0
and rank
\textnormal{{r}}=(r_{1},\cdots,r_{L})^{\top}\in\mathbb{N}^{L}
, if there is a t-NN
g(\underline{\textnormal{{x}}};{\underline{\textnormal{{W}}}}_{\textnormal{{r}}})\in\mathfrak{F}_{\textnormal{{r}}}
whose weights
{\underline{\textnormal{{W}}}}_{g}=(\textnormal{{w}},{\underline{\textnormal{{W}}}}_{r_{1}}^{(1)},\cdots,{\underline{\textnormal{{W}}}}_{r_{L}}^{(L)})
satisfy

\|{\underline{\textnormal{{W}}}}_{r_{l}}^{(l)}-{\underline{\textnormal{{W}}}}^{(l)}\|_{\textnormal{F}}\leq\delta
for any
l=1,\cdots,L
.

Furthermore, let’s consider the collection of t-NNs with approximately low-tubal-rank weights

\displaystyle\penalty\ \mathfrak{F}_{\delta,\textnormal{{r}}}:=\left\{f\in\mathfrak{F}\penalty\ |\penalty\ f\penalty\ \textnormal{satisfies the $(\delta,\textnormal{{r}})$-approximate low-tubal-rank parameterization}\right\}.

(15)

Subsequently, we analyze the AGP for any

f\in\mathfrak{F}_{\delta,\textnormal{{r}}}
in terms of its low-tubal-rank compression
g\in\mathfrak{F}_{\textnormal{{r}}}
. The idea is motivated by the work on compressed
bounds for non-compressed but compressible models
[
43
]
, originally developed for generalization analysis of NNs for standard training.

Under Assumption
2
, we first define

\mathfrak{F}^{\textnormal{adv}}_{\delta,\textnormal{{r}}}:=\{\tilde{f}:(\underline{\textnormal{{x}}},y)\mapsto\min_{R_{\textnormal{a}}(\underline{\textnormal{{x}}}^{\prime}-\underline{\textnormal{{x}}})\leq\xi}yf(\underline{\textnormal{{x}}}^{\prime})\penalty\ |\penalty\ f\in\mathfrak{F}_{\delta,\textnormal{{r}}}\}
as the adversarial version of
\mathfrak{F}_{\delta,\textnormal{{r}}}
.
To analyze the AGP of
f\in\mathfrak{F}_{\delta,\textnormal{{r}}}
through
g\in\mathfrak{F}_{\textnormal{{r}}}
, we instead consider their
adversarial counterparts
\tilde{f}\in\mathfrak{F}^{\textnormal{adv}}_{\delta,\textnormal{{r}}}
and
\tilde{g}\in\mathfrak{F}^{\textnormal{adv}}_{\textnormal{{r}}}
, where

\mathfrak{F}^{\textnormal{adv}}_{\textnormal{{r}}}
is defined as

\mathfrak{F}^{\textnormal{adv}}_{\textnormal{{r}}}:=\{\tilde{g}:(\underline{\textnormal{{x}}},y)\mapsto\min_{R_{\textnormal{a}}(\underline{\textnormal{{x}}}-\underline{\textnormal{{x}}}^{\prime})\leq\xi}yg(\underline{\textnormal{{x}}}^{\prime})\penalty\ \big|\penalty\ g\in\mathfrak{F}_{\textnormal{{r}}}\}.

Define the Minkowski difference of
\mathfrak{F}^{\textnormal{adv}}_{\delta,\textnormal{{r}}}
and
\mathfrak{F}^{\textnormal{adv}}_{\textnormal{{r}}}
as

\mathfrak{F}^{\textnormal{adv}}_{\delta,\textnormal{{r}}}-\mathfrak{F}^{\textnormal{adv}}_{\textnormal{{r}}}:=\{\tilde{f}-\tilde{g}\penalty\ |\penalty\ \tilde{f}\in\mathfrak{F}^{\textnormal{adv}}_{\delta,\textnormal{{r}}},\penalty\ \tilde{g}\in\mathfrak{F}^{\textnormal{adv}}_{\textnormal{{r}}}\}.

The empirical
L_{2}
-norm of a t-NN
h\in\mathfrak{F}
on the training data
S=\{(\underline{\textnormal{{x}}}_{i},y_{i})\}_{i=1}^{N}
is defined as
\|h\|_{S}:=\sqrt{N^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{N}h^{2}(\underline{\textnormal{{x}}}_{i},y_{i})}
, and the population
L_{2}
-norm is
\|h\|_{L_{2}}:=\sqrt{\mathbb{E}_{P(\underline{\textnormal{{x}}},y)}[h^{2}(\underline{\textnormal{{x}}},y)]}
. Define the local Rademacher complexity of
\mathfrak{F}^{\textnormal{adv}}_{\delta,\textnormal{{r}}}-\mathfrak{F}^{\textnormal{adv}}_{\textnormal{{r}}}
of radius
{\mathfrak{r}}>0

as

\dot{R}_{{\mathfrak{r}}}(\mathfrak{F}^{\textnormal{adv}}_{\delta,\textnormal{{r}}}-\mathfrak{F}^{\textnormal{adv}}_{\textnormal{{r}}}):=\bar{R}_{N}(\{h\in\mathfrak{F}^{\textnormal{adv}}_{\delta,\textnormal{{r}}}-\mathfrak{F}^{\textnormal{adv}}_{\textnormal{{r}}}\penalty\ |\penalty\ \left\|{h}\right\|_{L_{2}}\leq{\mathfrak{r}}\}),
where
\bar{R}_{N}({\cal H})
denotes the average Rademacher complexity of a function class
{\cal H}

[
4
]
.

The first part of the upcoming Theorem
12
shows that a small parametric distance between
f
and
g
leads to a small empirical
L_{2}
-distance in the adversarial output space. Specifically, for any
f(\underline{\textnormal{{x}}};{\underline{\textnormal{{W}}}})\in\mathfrak{F}_{\delta,\textnormal{{r}}}
with compression
g(\underline{\textnormal{{x}}};{\underline{\textnormal{{W}}}}_{\textnormal{{r}}})
, their (adversarial) empirical
L_{2}
-distance
\|\tilde{f}(\underline{\textnormal{{x}}};{\underline{\textnormal{{W}}}})-\tilde{g}(\underline{\textnormal{{x}}};{\underline{\textnormal{{W}}}}_{\textnormal{{r}}})\|_{S}
can be bounded by a small constant
\hat{{\mathfrak{r}}}>0
in linearity of
\delta
.
We also aim for a small population
L_{2}
-distance by first assuming the local Rademacher complexity
\dot{R}_{{\mathfrak{r}}}(\mathfrak{F}^{\textnormal{adv}}_{\delta,\textnormal{{r}}}-\mathfrak{F}^{\textnormal{adv}}_{\textnormal{{r}}})
can be bounded by a concave function of
{\mathfrak{r}}
, following common practice in Rademacher complexity analysis
[
4
,
43
]
.

Assumption 11
.

For any
{\mathfrak{r}}>0
, there exists a function
\phi({\mathfrak{r}}):[0,\infty)\rightarrow[0,\infty)
such that

\dot{R}_{{\mathfrak{r}}}(\mathfrak{F}^{\textnormal{adv}}_{\delta,\textnormal{{r}}}-\mathfrak{F}^{\textnormal{adv}}_{\textnormal{{r}}})\leq\phi({\mathfrak{r}})\penalty\ \penalty\ \textnormal{and}\penalty\ \penalty\ \phi(2{\mathfrak{r}})\leq 2\phi({\mathfrak{r}})
.

We further define

{\mathfrak{r}}_{*}={\mathfrak{r}}_{*}(t):=\inf\big\{{\mathfrak{r}}>0\penalty\ \big|16B_{\tilde{f}}{\mathfrak{r}}^{-2}\phi({\mathfrak{r}})+B_{\tilde{f}}{\mathfrak{r}}^{-1}\sqrt{2t/N}+2tB_{\tilde{f}}^{2}{\mathfrak{r}}^{-2}/N\leq 1/2\big\}
for any
t>0
,
such that
the population
L_{2}
-norm of
any
h\in\mathfrak{F}^{\textnormal{adv}}_{\delta,\textnormal{{r}}}-\mathfrak{F}^{\textnormal{adv}}_{\textnormal{{r}}}
can be bounded by

\|h\|_{L_{2}}^{2}\leq 2(\|h\|_{S}^{2}+{\mathfrak{r}}_{*}^{2})
using the peeling argument
[
42
, Theorem 7.7]
.
We then establish an adversarial generalization bound for approximately low-tubal-rank t-NNs as follows.

Theorem 12

(Adversarial generalization bound for general approximately
low-tubal-rank t-NNs)
.

(I). For any

f\in\mathfrak{F}_{\delta,\textnormal{{r}}}
with adversarial proxy
\tilde{f}\in\mathfrak{F}^{\textnormal{adv}}_{\delta,\textnormal{{r}}}
, there exists a function
g\in\mathfrak{F}_{\textnormal{{r}}}
with adversarial proxy

\tilde{g}\in\mathfrak{F}^{\textnormal{adv}}_{\textnormal{{r}}}
, such that the empirical
L_{2}
-distance
\|\tilde{f}-\tilde{g}\|_{S}\leq\delta B_{\tilde{f}}\sum_{l=1}^{L}B_{l}^{-1}=:\hat{{\mathfrak{r}}}
.

(II). Let
\dot{{\mathfrak{r}}}:=\sqrt{2(\hat{{\mathfrak{r}}}^{2}+{\mathfrak{r}}_{*}^{2})}
. Under Assumptions
1
,
2
,
4
,

11
, there exist constants

C_{1},C_{2}>0
satisfying

\displaystyle\penalty\ \mathcal{L}^{\textnormal{adv}}(f)-\hat{\mathcal{L}}^{\textnormal{adv}}(f)

\displaystyle\leq\underbrace{\frac{C_{1}L_{\ell}B_{\tilde{f}}}{\sqrt{N}}\sqrt{{\sf c}\sum_{l=1}^{L}r_{l}(d_{l-1}+d_{l})\log(9(L+1))}+B\sqrt{\frac{t}{2N}}}_{\textnormal{main term}}

(16)

\displaystyle+\underbrace{C_{2}\left(\Phi(\dot{{\mathfrak{r}}})+L_{\ell}\dot{{\mathfrak{r}}}\sqrt{\frac{t}{N}}+\frac{tL_{\ell}B_{\tilde{f}}}{N}\right)}_{\textnormal{bias term}},

for any
f\in\mathfrak{F}_{\delta,\textnormal{{r}}}
with probability at least

1-4e^{-t}
for any
t>0
, where

\Phi({\mathfrak{r}})
is defined as

\displaystyle\penalty\ \Phi({\mathfrak{r}}):=\bar{R}_{N}\left(\big\{\ell\circ\tilde{f}-\ell\circ\tilde{g}\penalty\ \big|\penalty\ \tilde{f}\in\mathfrak{F}^{\textnormal{adv}}_{\delta,\textnormal{{r}}},\tilde{g}\in\mathfrak{F}^{\textnormal{adv}}_{\textnormal{{r}}},\|\tilde{f}-\tilde{g}\|_{L_{2}}\leq{\mathfrak{r}}\big\}\right).

The main term of the bound quantifies the complexity of functions in
\mathfrak{F}_{\textnormal{{r}}}
with exact low-tubal-rank parameterization in adversarial settings, which can be significantly smaller than that of
\mathfrak{F}_{\delta,\textnormal{{r}}}
. On the other hand, the bias term captures the sample complexity required to bridge the gap between approximately low-tubal-rank parameterized
\mathfrak{F}_{\delta,\textnormal{{r}}}
and exactly low-tubal-rank parameterized
\mathfrak{F}_{\textnormal{{r}}}
. As we usually observe
{\mathfrak{r}}_{*}^{2}=o(1/\sqrt{N})
, setting
\hat{{\mathfrak{r}}}=o_{p}(1)
allows the bias term to decay faster than the main term, which is
O(1/\sqrt{N})
.
Theorem
12
suggests that
a small parametric distance between
f\in\mathfrak{F}_{\delta,\textnormal{{r}}}
and
g\in\mathfrak{F}_{\textnormal{{r}}}
also implies a small difference in their adversarial generalization behaviors
.

A special case.

We also showcase a specific scenario where the weights of t-product layers exhibit a polynomial spectral decay in the transformed domain, leading to a considerably small AGP bound.

Assumption 13
.

Consider the setting where any t-NN
f(\underline{\textnormal{{x}}};{\underline{\textnormal{{W}}}})\in\mathfrak{F}_{\delta,\textnormal{{r}}}
has tensor weights
{\underline{\textnormal{{W}}}}^{(l)}\penalty\ (l=1,\cdots,L)
whose singular values in the transformed domain satisfy
\sigma_{j}(M({\underline{\textnormal{{W}}}}^{(l)})_{:,:,k})\leq V_{0}\cdot j^{-\alpha}
, where
V_{0}>0
is a constant, and
\sigma_{j}(\cdot)
is the
j
-th largest singular value of a matrix.

Under Assumption
13
, the weight tensor
{\underline{\textnormal{{W}}}}^{(l)}
can be approximated by
its optimal tubal-rank-
r_{l}
approximation

{\underline{\textnormal{{W}}}}^{(l)}_{r_{l}}
for any
1\leq r_{l}\leq\min\{d_{l},d_{l-1}\}
with error

\|{\underline{\textnormal{{W}}}}^{(l)}-{\underline{\textnormal{{W}}}}^{(l)}_{r_{l}}\|_{\textnormal{F}}\leq\sqrt{{{\sf c}}/{(2\alpha-1)}}V_{0}(r_{l}-1)^{(1-2\alpha)/2}

[
20
]
,
which can be much smaller than

\|{\underline{\textnormal{{W}}}}^{(l)}_{r_{l}}\|_{\textnormal{F}}

when
\alpha>1/2
is sufficiently large.
Thus, we can find an exactly low-tubal-rank parameterized
g\in\mathfrak{F}_{\textnormal{{r}}}
for any
f\in\mathfrak{F}_{\delta,\textnormal{{r}}}
satisfying Assumption
13
, such that the parametric distance between
g
and
f
is quite small.
The following theorem shows that the small parametric distance also leads to a small AGP.

Theorem 14
.

Under Assumptions
1
,
2
,
4
, and
13
, if we let

\hat{{\mathfrak{r}}}=V_{0}B_{\tilde{f}}\sum_{l=1}^{L}(r_{l}+1)^{-\alpha}B_{l}^{-1},

then for any t-NN
f\in\mathfrak{F}_{\delta,\textnormal{{r}}}
, there exists a function
g\in\mathfrak{F}_{\textnormal{{r}}}
whose t-product layer weights have tubal-rank exactly no greater than
r_{l}
, satisfying
\|\tilde{f}-\tilde{g}\|_{S}\leq\hat{{\mathfrak{r}}}
.
Further, there is a constant
C_{\alpha}
only depending on
\alpha
such that the AGP, i.e.,
\mathcal{L}^{\textnormal{adv}}(f)-\hat{\mathcal{L}}^{\textnormal{adv}}(f)
, of any
f\in\mathfrak{F}_{\delta,\textnormal{{r}}}
can be upper bounded by

\displaystyle\penalty\ C_{\alpha}L_{\ell}\bigg\{B_{\tilde{f}}E_{1}+\hat{{\mathfrak{r}}}\sqrt{E_{1}}+E_{2}^{\frac{2\alpha}{2\alpha+1}}\left(B_{\tilde{f}}^{\frac{2\alpha-1}{2\alpha+1}}+1\right)+\hat{{\mathfrak{r}}}^{\frac{2\alpha}{2\alpha+1}}\sqrt{E_{2}}+(\hat{{\mathfrak{r}}}+\frac{B}{L_{\ell}})\sqrt{\frac{t}{N}}+\frac{1+tB_{\tilde{f}}}{N}\bigg\},

for any
t>0
with probability at least
1-4e^{-t}
, where
E_{1}=N^{-1}{\sf c}\sum_{l=1}^{L}r_{l}(d_{l}+d_{l-1})\log(9NLB_{\tilde{f}}/\sqrt{{\sf c}})
and

E_{2}=N^{-1}{\sf c}\sum_{l=1}^{L}\Big(LV_{0}B_{\tilde{f}}B_{l}^{-1}\Big)^{1/\alpha}(d_{l}+d_{l-1})\log(9NLB_{\tilde{f}}/\sqrt{{\sf c}})
.

This suggests that by choosing a sufficiently large
\alpha>1/2
, where each weight tensor has a tubal-rank close to 1, we can attain a superior generalization error bound. It is important to note that the rank
r_{l}
can be arbitrarily chosen, and there exists a trade-off relationship between
\hat{{\mathfrak{r}}}
and
E_{1}
. Therefore, by selecting the rank appropriately for a balanced trade-off, we can obtain an optimal bound as follows.

Corollary 15
.

Under the same assumption to Theorem
14
,
if we choose the parameter
r
of tubal ranks in
\mathfrak{F}_{\textnormal{{r}}}
by
r_{l}=\min\{\lceil\big(LV_{0}B_{\tilde{f}}B_{l}^{-1}\big)^{1/\alpha}\rceil,d_{l},d_{l-1}\}
, then there is a constant
C_{\alpha}
only depending on
\alpha
such that the AGP of any
f\in\mathfrak{F}_{\delta,\textnormal{{r}}}
can be upper bounded as

\displaystyle\penalty\ \mathcal{L}^{\textnormal{adv}}(f)-\hat{\mathcal{L}}^{\textnormal{adv}}(f)\leq C_{\alpha}L_{\ell}\bigg\{

\displaystyle B_{\tilde{f}}^{1-1/(2\alpha)}\sqrt{\frac{{\sf c}\sum_{l=1}^{L}\Big(LV_{0}B_{l}^{-1}\Big)^{1/\alpha}(d_{l}+d_{l-1})\log(9NLB_{\tilde{f}}/\sqrt{{\sf c}})}{N}}

\displaystyle+E_{2}^{\frac{2\alpha}{2\alpha+1}}\left(B_{\tilde{f}}^{\frac{2\alpha-1}{2\alpha+1}}+1\right)+\sqrt{E_{2}}+\frac{B}{L_{\ell}}\sqrt{\frac{t}{N}}+\frac{1+tB_{\tilde{f}}}{N}\bigg\},

with probability at least
1-4e^{-t}
for any
t>0
.

It is worth highlighting that the bound exhibits a linear dependency on the number of neurons in the t-product layers, represented as
O(\sqrt{{{\sf c}\sum_{l}(d_{l}+d_{l-1})}/{N}})
. In contrast, Theorem
5
demonstrates a dependency on the total number of parameters, denoted as
O(\sqrt{{{\sf c}\sum_{l}d_{l}d_{l-1}}/{N}})
. This observation suggests that employing the low-tubal-rank parameterization can potentially enhance adversarial generalization for t-NNs.

5
Related Works

T-SVD-based data and function representation.

The unique feature of t-SVD-based data representation, in contrast to classical low-rank decomposition methods, is the presence of low-rankness in the transformed domain. This transformed low-rankness is crucial for effectively modeling real multi-channel data with both smoothness and low-rankness
[
24
,
50
,
49
]
. Utilized in t-product layers in DNNs
[
36
,
32
,
53
]
, t-SVD has also been a workhorse for function representation and achieves impressive empirical performance. While t-SVD-based signal processing models have been extensively studied theoretically
[
60
,
24
,
40
,
50
,
13
,
27
]
, the t-SVD-based learning model itself has not been thoroughly scrutinized until this paper. Hence, this study represents the first theoretical analysis of t-SVD-based learning models, contributing to the understanding of their theoretical foundations.

Theoretical analysis methods.
Our analysis draws on norm-based generalization analysis
[
37
]
and implicit regularization of gradient descent-based learning
[
46
]
as related theoretical analysis methods.
Norm-based generalization analysis plays a crucial role in theoretical analysis across various domains, including standard generalization analysis of DNNs
[
8
]
, compressed models
[
22
]
, non-compressed models
[
43
]
, and adversarial generalization analysis
[
59
,
3
,
55
]
.
Our work extends norm-based tools to analyze both standard and adversarial generalization in t-NNs, going beyond the traditional use of matrix products. For implicit regularization of gradient descent based learning, extensive past research has been conducted on implicit bias of GF for both standard and adversarial training of homogeneous networks building on matrix product layers, respectively
[
30
,
16
,
45
]
.
We non-trivially extend these methods to analyze t-NNs and reveals that GF for over-parameterized ReLU t-NNs produces nearly transformed low-rank weights under scale invariant adversarial perturbations.

Our theoretical results notably deviate from the standard error bounds for fully connected neural networks (FNNs) in several ways:

•

The generalization bounds in Lemma
3
and Theorem
5
for t-NNs diverge from their counterparts for FNNs in Refs.
[
8
,
55
,
59
]
due to the channel number
{\sf c}
in t-NNs. Moreover, Theorem
5
encompasses a wider range of adversary classes than the
l_{p}
-attacks in the aforementioned references.

•

The uniqueness of Theorem
6
, compared to Refs.
[
55
,
59
]
, stems from its consideration of weight low-rankness in the adversarial generalization bound, suggesting possible robustness improvements in generalization.

•

Our exploration of the implicit bias in GF for adversarial training presents a novel angle: the bias towards approximate transformed low-rankness in t-NNs. While Ref.
[
29
]
focuses on the implicit bias in adversarial training for FNNs, centered on KKT point convergence with exponential loss, our work delves deeper, considering a wider array of loss functions in adversarial training for t-NNs.

•

A crucial distinction in our adversarial generalization bounds, detailed in Section
4.3
, from non-adversarial bounds for FNNs
[
43
]
is the integration of the localized Rademacher complexity. This encompasses the Minkowski difference between adversarial counterparts of both approximately and exactly low-tubal-rank t-NNs as seen in Theorem
12
.

6
Concluding Remarks

A thorough investigation of the generalization behavior of t-NNs is conducted for the first time. We derive upper bounds for the generalization gaps of standard and adversarially trained t-NNs and propose compressing t-NNs with a transformed low-rank structure for more efficient adversarial learning and tighter bounds on the adversarial generalization gap. Our analysis shows that adversarial training with GF in highly over-parameterized settings results in t-NNs with approximately transformed low-rank weights. We further establish sharp adversarial generalization bounds for t-NNs with approximately transformed low-rank weights. Our findings demonstrate that utilizing the transformed low-rank parameterization can significantly enhance the robust generalization of t-NNs, carrying both theoretical and empirical significance.

Limitations.

While this paper adheres to the norm-based framework for capacity control
[
37
,
8
]
, it is worth noting that the obtained generalization bounds may be somewhat conservative. However, this limitation can be mitigated by employing more sophisticated analysis techniques, as evidenced by recent studies
[
2
,
25
,
57
,
56
]
.

Discussions.
The inclination of adversarial training towards low-rank/sparse weights, and the reciprocal effects of parameter reduction on robustness, are currently at the forefront of ongoing research. This domain has witnessed a spectrum of observations and results
[
51
,
6
,
41
,
23
]
. In this study, we propose that employing low-rank parameterization can enhance the adversarial robustness of t-NNs, as evidenced by our analysis of uniform adversarial generalization error bounds. However, despite these promising results, it is crucial to emphasize the necessity of a more exhaustive exploration of low-rank parameterization. Its implications, particularly when considered in the context of approximation, estimation, and optimization, are profound and warrant further dedicated research efforts. Such a comprehensive investigation will undoubtedly enhance our understanding and fully unlock the potential of low-rank parameterization in neural networks.

Appendix

Transformed Low-Rank Parameterization Can Help

Robust Generalization for Tensor Neural Networks

In the appendix, we begin by presenting numerical evaluations of our theoretical findings. Subsequently, we introduce the additional notations and preliminaries related to t-SVD, followed by the proofs of the propositions mentioned in the main text.

Our analysis and proofs pertaining to t-NNs differ from those for FNNs as follows:

•

Firstly, unlike the analysis for FNNs’ generalization bounds based on Rademacher complexity in Refs.
[
8
,
55
,
59
]
, we derive specific lemmas for standard and adversarial generalization bounds in t-NNs. This is due to the unique structure of (low-rank) t-product layers. We reformulate the t-product through an operator-like expression in Lemma 16, paving the way for pivotal Lemma 17, supporting the t-product-based “peeling argument.” Additionally, we introduce Lemmas 37, 38, and Lemma 33 to handle t-product layer output norms and covering low-tubal-rank tensors.

•

Secondly, proving the implicit bias of GF for adversarial training of t-NNs, specifically the approximately transformed low-rankness, is nontrivial in comparison to the proof in Ref.
[
29
]
for the implicit bias of adversarial training for FNNs. As we consider more general loss functions for t-NNs in contrast to the exponential loss for FNNs in Ref.
[
29
]
, we first derive a more general convergence result to the direction of a KKT point for t-NNs Lemma
9
, and then goes deeper by using a constructive approach to establish the approximately transformed low-rankness in
Theorem
10
.

•

Thirdly, differing from Ref.
[
43
]
which focuses on standard FNN generalization, our approach delves into t-NNs’ adversarial generalization. We achieve this by introducing the
(\delta,\textnormal{{r}})
-parameterization, bounding localized Rademacher complexity for a Minkowski set in adversarial settings, and using low-tubal-rank approximations for tensor weights.

Appendix A
Numerical Evaluations of the Theoretical Results

This section presents numerical evaluations for our theoretical results. All training process is conducted on nVidia A100 GPU. For additional information and access to the demo code, please visit the following URL:
https://github.com/pingzaiwang/Analysis4TNN/
.

A.1
Effects of Exact Transformed Low-rank Weights on the Adversarial Generalization Gap

To validate the adversarial generalization bound in Theorem
6
, we have conducted experiments on the MNIST dataset to explore the relationship between adversarial generalization gaps (AGP), weight tensor low-rankness, and training sample size. We consider binary classification of 3 and 7, with FSGM
[
9
]
attacks of strength
20/255
. The t-NN consists of three t-product layers and one FC layer, with weight tensor dimensions of
28\times 28\times 28
for
{\underline{\textnormal{{W}}}}^{(1)}
,
{\underline{\textnormal{{W}}}}^{(2)}
, and
{\underline{\textnormal{{W}}}}^{(3)}
, and
784
for the FC weight
w
. As an input to the t-NN, each MNIST image of size
28\times 28
is treated as a t-vector of size
28\times 1\times 28
.

(a)

(b)

Figure 1:
The adversarial generalization gaps plotted against the training sample size (
N
) for t-NNs, both with and without transformed low-rank weight tensors, using the MNIST dataset. In (a), the adversarial generalization gaps are presented against the sample size
N
, while in (b), they are plotted against
1/\sqrt{N}
.

Theorem
6
emphasizes: (
i
) lower weight tensor rank leads to smaller bounds on the adversarial generalization gaps, and (
ii
) the bound diminishes at a rate of
O(1/\sqrt{N})
as
N
increases. We explored this by conducting experiments, controlling the upper bounds of the tubal-rank to 4 and 28 for low and full tubal-rank cases, and systematically increasing the number of training samples.

Fig.
1
presents the results. The curves indicate that t-NNs with lower rank weight tensors have smaller robust generalization errors. Interestingly, the adversarial generalization errors seem to follow a linear relationship with
1/\sqrt{N}
, approximately validating the generalization error bound in Theorem 6 by approximating the scaling behavior of the empirical errors.

A.2
Implicit Bias of GF-based Adversarial Training to Approximately Transformed Low-rank Weight Tensors

We carried out experiments to confirm two theoretical statements related to the analysis of GF-based adversarial training.

Statement A.2.1
Theorem
10
reveals that, under specific conditions, well-trained t-NNs with highly over-parameterized adversarial training using GF show nearly transformed low-rank parameters.

Statement A.2.2
Lemma 22 asserts that the empirical adversarial risk approaches zero, and the F-norm of the weights grows infinitely as
t
approaches infinity.

In continuation of the experimental settings in Sec.
A.1
, we focus on binary classification on MNIST under FGSM attacks. The t-NN is structured with three t-product layers and one FC layer, with weight dimensions set to
D\times 28\times 28
for
{\underline{\textnormal{{W}}}}^{(1)}
,
D\times D\times 28
for
{\underline{\textnormal{{W}}}}^{(2)}
and
{\underline{\textnormal{{W}}}}^{(3)}
, and
28D
for the FC weight
w
. Our experiments involve setting values of
D
to
128
and
256
, respectively, and we track the effective rank of each weight tensor, the empirical adversarial risk, and the F-norm of the weights as the number of epochs progresses. Since implementing gradient flow with infinitely small step size is impractical in real experiments, we opt for SGD with a constant learning rate and batch-size of
80
, following the setting on fully connected layers in Ref.
[
29
]
.

For Statement A.2.1, we present preliminary results illustrating the progression of the stable ranks of the
M
-block-diagonal matrix of tensor weights in Fig.
2
for the settings
D\in\{128,256\}
. Notably, these results show that the effective ranks decrease as more epochs are executed, thereby confirming the influence of implicit bias on transformed low-rankness, as described in Statement A.2.1.

For Statement A.2.2, we present initial numerical findings depicting the progress of the empirical adversarial risk and the F-norm of the weights in Figs.
3
and
4
, respectively. These results exhibit a consistent pattern with the theoretical descriptions outlined in Statement A.2.2 and the numerical results reported in Ref.
[
29
]
for adversarial training and Ref.
[
30
]
for standard training of FNNs. Specifically, we observe a decreasing trend in the empirical risk function and an increasing trend in the weight tensor’s F-norm, which align with the expected behavior based on our theoretical framework and corroborate the numerical results presented in Refs.
[
29
,
30
]
.

A.3
Additional Regularization for a Better Low-rank Parameterized t-NN

It is natural to ask:
instead of using adversarial training with GF in highly over-parameterized settings to train a approximately transformed low-rank t-NN, is it possible to apply some extra regularizations in training to achieve a better low-rank parameterization
?

Yes, it is possible to apply additional regularizations during training to achieve a better low-rank representation in t-NNs. Instead of relying solely on adversarial training with gradient flow in highly over-parameterized settings, these extra regularizations can potentially promote and enforce low-rankness in the network.

To validate the concern regarding the addition of an extra regularization term, we performed a preliminary experiment. In this experiment, we incorporated the tubal nuclear norm
[
48
]
as an explicit regularizer to induce low-rankness in the transformed domain. Specifically, we add the tubal nuclear norm regularization to the t-NN with three t-product layer
D=128
in Sec.
A.2
with a regularization parameter
0.01
, and keep the other settings the same as Sec.
A.2
. We explore how the stable ranks of tensor weights evolve with the epoch number with/without tubal nuclear norm regularization.

The experimental results are depicted in Fig.
5
. According to Fig.
5
, it becomes evident that the introduction of the explicit low-rank regularizer significantly enforced low-rankness in the transform domain of the weight tensors.

Figure 5:
Curves of the stable ranks of the
M
-block-diagonal matrix of the weight tensors versus the epoch number in the scenario where
D=128
, both with and without the tubal nuclear norm regularization, using the MNIST dataset.

B.2
Additional Preliminaries of t-SVD

We give additional notions and propositions about t-SVD omitted in the main body
of the paper.

Definition 6

(
[
18
]
)
.

The t-transpose of
{\underline{\textnormal{{T}}}}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n\times{\sf c}}
under the
M
transform in Eq. (
1
), denoted by
{\underline{\textnormal{{T}}}}^{\top}
, satisfies

M({\underline{\textnormal{{T}}}}^{\top})_{:,:,k}=(M({\underline{\textnormal{{T}}}})_{:,:,k})^{\top},\penalty\ k=1,\cdots,{\sf c}.

Definition 7

(
[
18
]
)
.

The t-identity tensor
{\underline{\textnormal{{I}}}}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times m\times{\sf c}}
under
M
transform in Eq. (
1
) is the tensor such that each frontal slice of
M({\underline{\textnormal{{I}}}})
is a
{\sf c}\times{\sf c}
identity matrix,i.e,

M({\underline{\textnormal{{I}}}})_{:,:,k}=\textnormal{{I}},\penalty\ k=1,\cdots,{\sf c}.

Given the appropriate dimensions, it is trivial to verify that
{\underline{\textnormal{{T}}}}*_{M}{\underline{\textnormal{{I}}}}={\underline{\textnormal{{T}}}}
and
{\underline{\textnormal{{I}}}}*_{M}{\underline{\textnormal{{T}}}}={\underline{\textnormal{{T}}}}
.

Definition 8

(
[
18
]
)
.

A tensor
{\underline{\textnormal{{Q}}}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d\times d_{3}}
is t-orthogonal under
M
transform in Eq. (
1
) if it satisfies

\displaystyle\penalty\ {\underline{\textnormal{{Q}}}}^{\top}*_{M}{\underline{\textnormal{{Q}}}}={\underline{\textnormal{{Q}}}}*_{M}{\underline{\textnormal{{Q}}}}^{\top}={\underline{\textnormal{{I}}}}.

Definition 9

(
[
19
]
)
.

A tensor is called f-diagonal if all its frontal slices are diagonal matrices.

Definition 10

(Tensor t-spectral norm
[
28
]
)
.

The tensor t-spectral norm of any tensor
{\underline{\textnormal{{T}}}}
under
M
transform in Eq. (
1
) is defined as the matrix spectral norm of its
M
-block-diagonal matrix
\widetilde{{\underline{\textnormal{{T}}}}}_{M}
, i.e.,

\displaystyle\penalty\ \left\|{{\underline{\textnormal{{T}}}}}\right\|_{\textnormal{sp}}:=\left\|{\widetilde{\textnormal{{T}}}_{M}}\right\|.

Lemma 16
.

For any t-matrix
{\underline{\textnormal{{W}}}}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n\times{\sf c}}
and t-vector
\underline{\textnormal{{x}}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times 1\times c}
, the t-product
{\underline{\textnormal{{W}}}}*_{M}\underline{\textnormal{{x}}}
defined under
M
transform in Eq. (
1
)
is equivalent to a linear operator
{\textnormal{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\footnotesize\small{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{op}}}}}}}({\underline{\textnormal{{W}}}})
on
{\textnormal{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\footnotesize\small{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{unfold}}}}}}}(\underline{\textnormal{{x}}})
in the orginal domain defined as follows

\displaystyle{{{{\displaystyle\penalty\ \textnormal{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\footnotesize\small{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{op}}}}}}}({\underline{\textnormal{{W}}}})(\underline{\textnormal{{x}}})=(\textnormal{{M}}^{-1}\otimes\textnormal{{I}}_{m})\bigg[\textnormal{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\footnotesize\small{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{bdiag}}}}}}}\big((\textnormal{{M}}\otimes\textnormal{{I}}_{m})\textnormal{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\footnotesize\small{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{unfold}}}}}}}({\underline{\textnormal{{W}}}})\big)(\textnormal{{M}}\otimes\textnormal{{I}}_{n})\bigg]\textnormal{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\footnotesize\small{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{unfold}}}}}}}(\underline{\textnormal{{x}}}),

(17)

where
\otimes
denotes the Kronecker product, and the operations of
{\textnormal{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\footnotesize\small{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{unfold}}}}}}}({\underline{\textnormal{{W}}}})
and
